## Page 1


WELL-POSEDNESS OF A GENERALIZED STOKES OPERATOR
ON DOMAINS WITH CYLINDRICAL ENDS VIA
LAYER-POTENTIALS
MIRELA KOHR, VICTOR NISTOR, AND WOLFGANG L. WENDLAND
In memory of Professor Gabriela Kohr, with deep respect
Abstract. We study the generalized Stokes operator
Ξ := ΞV,V0 :=
 L + V
∇
∇∗
−V0

on a domain with straight cylindrical ends Ωusing the method of layer po-
tentials on a larger manifold with straight cylindrical ends M, Ω⊂M. The
operator Ξ := ΞV,V0 recovers the classical Stokes operator Ξ0,0 when the
potentials V and V0 vanish. Under suitable positivity assumptions at infinity
on V and V0, we prove that Ξ is Fredholm on L2(M; TM ⊕C). This allows us
then to define the single- and double-layer potential operators S and 1
2 + K.
Under further positivity assumptions at infinity, we prove that S and 1
2 +K are
also Fredholm. Under slightly stronger assumptions on V and V0 (including
positivity everywhere), we prove the invertibility of the operators Ξ, S, and
1
2 + K. As it is well-known, the invertibility of these operators leads to well-
posedness results for the associated (linear) Stokes boundary value problem
with Dirichlet boundary conditions on domains with straight cylindrical ends.
The proofs of these results required us to develop many other related tools. In
particular, we develop an “algebra tool kit” to deal with limit and jump rela-
tions of layer potential operators, in general, and on manifolds with straight
cylindrical ends, in particular. We do that first on Rn, then on (possibly) non-
compact manifolds, and then we deal with the specific case of manifolds with
straight cylindrical ends. We use these results to study the limit and jump
relations of the layer potential operators associated to our generalized Stokes
operator Ξ := ΞV,V0 on manifolds with cylindrical ends.
We also develop
Green formulas and energy estimates for our generalized Stokes operator Ξ on
manifolds with straight cylindrical ends, which requires, in particular, a careful
geometric study of the related differential operators, such as the deformation
operator Def . We also use suitable classes of pseudodifferential operators on
manifolds with straight cylindrical ends that were studied in some previous
papers of ours (including “The Stokes operator on manifolds with cylindrical
ends,” J. Diff. Equations, 2024). For completeness, we include a review of
the needed results on these pseudodifferential operators. As an application of
all these results, we prove the well-posedness result for the Dirichlet problem
for the generalized Navier-Stokes system with small data on a domain with
cylindrical ends. We expect our results to have applications to the study of
Navier-Stokes equations on domains with conical points.

1. Introduction
We prove the well-posedness of the Dirichlet boundary value problem for a gen-
eralized Stokes system (1.4) on a domain with straight cylindrical ends Ωusing the
method of layer potentials. In the process, we obtain many other results of inde-
pendent interest on the generalized Stokes operator Ξ := ΞV,V0 (1.3), on related
differential operators, and on their associated layer potentials. We carefully develop
the needed concepts and methods and prove in detail our results. Before stating in
more detail our main results, let us introduce the setting of our paper.
1.1. The setting of our well-posedness results. We will work on manifolds
and domains with straight cylindrical ends (see the next figure, Figure 1, as well as
Figure 2 at the beginning of Section 6). More precisely, by a manifold with straight

12.2 following [69]). Let us thus assume in this introduction that M = M0 ∪

that they have the usual principal symbols (Theorem 8.16; but in order to obtain

geometric representations and formulas for our operators, a “took-kit” for proving
jump relations, and many other results of independent interest). Then, using the
jump relations and principal symbol properties of the layer potential operators, we
prove that S and 1

solvability of the Dirichlet problem (1.4), Theorem 1.1 next, which is the main
result of this paper. We write v ≻0 for a non-negative function that does not

the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator and to the solvability of the stationary Navier-
Stokes system on domains with straight cylindrical ends of small dimension.
We have outlined here the most important results. However, in this paper, we
obtain many other results of independent interest that lead to the main results.
These other results are discussed in much more detail in the part on the contents
of the paper, which is next.
1.3. Contents of the paper. Our paper consists of 12.1 sections (including an
introduction), each divided into subsections. The assumptions of this paper (espe-
cially on the manifold M on which we work) change from section to section. These
assumptions are always stated at the beginning of that section and are reminded
regularly, especially at the beginning of the subsections and in the statement of the


## Page 6


6
M. KOHR, V. NISTOR, AND W.L. WENDLAND
most important results. (Note, however, that, beginning with Section 5, we work
almost exclusively on manifolds with straight cylindrical ends.)
The first section is this introduction. Section 2 is organized as follows. Its first
subsection, Subsection 2.1, is devoted to the review of some classical differential
operators that appear in the definition and in the study of the Stokes operator
Ξ = ΞV,V0 on a Riemannian manifold (recall that ΞV,V0 was introduced in Equation
(1.3)), such as the deformation operator Def and the “traction” T ν. In Subsection
2.2, we recall an abstract integration by parts formula, Proposition 2.9. This general
formula is used in Subsection 2.3, where we obtain Green-type formulas for the
Stokes operator Ξ, as well as some representation formulas and energy estimates.
In this section, we work on a general Riemannian manifold M.
The third section is devoted to some basic preliminary results such as the con-
cepts of traces and normal lateral limits, some useful Fourier transforms, the basic
definitions of symbols and pseudodifferential operators.
In particular, they ex-
plain in detail the main “Fourier transform calculation” that is the prototypical
“jump/limit result”, see Lemma 3.3 and Corollary 3.4.
These two results ulti-
mately are responsible and explain all the other jump/limit results. These results
are formulated on Rn. However, in the last subsection of this section, we work
on manifolds with straight cylindrical ends. In this subsection, we recall the basic
definitions related to manifolds with cylindrical ends and their compatible vector
bundles, differential operators, and Sobolev spaces. Although the results of this
section are classical, our presentation is new. (More references and more related
background material can be found in our book [63].)
In the forth section, we discuss the jump relations for pseudodifferential opera-
tors a(x, D) at the hyperplane {xn = 0} in Rn. This is a long and technical section
in which we perform in great detail all the necessary local calculations needed to
obtain the jump and limit relations that are so important for the study of layer
potentials. We first establish the jump relations for operators of order m < −1 (the
“jump” in this case is zero, so these are rather “limit” results). We prove our results
for operators with Kohn-Nirenberg type symbols. We then establish the jump rela-
tions and the other usual results for operators of order −1 with homogeneous, odd
principal symbol. The presentation expands and details the one in our book [63].
For instance, in this paper, we do not restrict ourselves to classical pseudodifferen-
tial operators (however, for operators of order −1, we give our results directly in the
case of an odd symbol, the only one needed in our paper). Our presentation in this
section provides a complete, yet concise, new introduction to the subject of limit
and jump relations for potential operators on a half-space. They are the first step in
the development of an “algebra tool kit” to study the limit/jump relations. These
results, together with those in Section 6 provide a short–but complete–introduction
to jump relations on manifolds with cylindrical ends. (Results for manifolds with
bounded geometry can be found in [69].)
The setting of our paper, up to this point, was on Rn or on a general Riemannian
manifold (except Subsection 3.4, where we defined manifolds with straight cylin-
drical ends). Beginning with the next section, Section 5, however, we will work
almost exclusively on manifolds with straight cylindrical ends.
(Sometimes, we
need to consider the particular case of straight cylinders.) In particular, Section
5 is devoted to pseudodifferential operators on manifolds with straight cylindrical
ends. Here we introduce the main two classes of pseudodifferential operators used


## Page 7


WELL-POSEDNESS FOR A GENERALIZED STOKES OPERATOR
7
in this paper. The differential operators considered in this paper will belong to the
first class, while their parametrices and layer potential operators will belong to the
second of these classes. More precisely, in the first half of this section, we recall the
definition and main properties of the inv-calculus of translation invariant operators
in a neighborhood of infinity, and, in the second half we recall the definition and
the main properties of the ess-calculus introduced in [67] (see also [63, 68]). This
calculus contains the inv-calculus, but it has further important properties due to
the fact that it contains the parametrices of its operators (i.e., it is spectrally in-
variant). Since this material is well-known, the proofs are short. However, all the
necessary definitions and properties are discussed in great detail. In particular, we
introduce the “limit operator” R∞(P) associated to an operator P in any of our
two calculi. The limit operator R∞(P) is a translation invariant operator on the
infinite cylinder M ′ × R associated to our manifold with straight cylindrical ends
M = M0 ∪

M ′ ×(−∞, 0)

. The concept of limit operator is crucial for the study of
operators on manifolds with cylindrical ends. We also introduce the indicial family
bP(τ), which is the Fourier transform of R∞(P). See [47, 84, 94, 124] for the closely
related b-calculus.
In the sixth section, we extend the “jump/limit’ results of the forth section to
the case of Riemannian manifolds with straight cylindrical ends. We thus study
here the normal lateral limits on manifolds with straight cylindrical ends. To this
end, we develop an “algebra tool kit” to study the limit/jump relations. We prove,
for example, that normal lateral limits are compatible with limits at infinity (i.e.,
with the limit operator map R∞), a result that was not published before in a
refereed journal (to our knowledge), see Theorem 6.10. This result is key in the
study of the layer potentials in the remaining sections. In order to define the normal
lateral limits on manifolds, we need to develop some geometric tools, including the
normal geodesics, and to make some corresponding geometric assumptions, which
we prove are satisfied for our manifolds with cylindrical ends.
Our generalized
Stokes operator Ξ is a differential operator of order two, but is not elliptic in the
usual sense. In order to use the theory of elliptic operators, we recall the definition
of Douglis-Nirenberg-operators and ellipticity in this setting in the last subsection of
section. The normal lateral limit results extend right away to Douglis-Nirenberg-
operators. The content of this section is motivated by the need to establish the
Douglis-Nirenberg-ellipticity of the Stokes operator Ξ. Our results extend those in
[63, 69].
The seventh section is devoted to the study of some of the main technical results
on a cylinder.
We begin by studying the form of our differential operators (Ξ,
Def , and ∇) on a cylinder S × R (which is a particular case of a manifold with
cylindrical ends), where S is a boundaryless, smooth, compact manifold (i.e., a
closed manifold). Then we extend the results of Section 2 on Green formulas and
energy estimates to the cylinder S×R. We also extend the Green formulas and the
energy estimates of Section 2 to the indicial family bΞ(τ) of Ξ. As an application, we
obtain criteria for the invertibility of the translation invariant, generalized Stokes
operator Ξ on the cylinder S × R, under suitable positivity assumptions on V and
V0 (Assumption 8.1).
In Section 8, we begin the study of the main operator of this paper, namely, the
generalized Stokes operator Ξ := ΞV,V0 (see Equation (1.3)) acting on a manifold
with straight cylindrical ends. (Recall that, if V and V0 vanish, then Ξ becomes the


## Page 8


8
M. KOHR, V. NISTOR, AND W.L. WENDLAND
usual Stokes operator.) We first obtain Fredholm and invertibility results for the
Stokes operator Ξ under suitable positivity conditions on the potentials V and V0
(Assumption 8.1 and, respectively, Assumption 8.3). These results are interesting
in themselves. Moreover, they play a crucial role in our paper, as these Fredholm
and invertibility results are used then (in this section) to define the various layer
potentials for the Stokes operator. In the last subsection of this section, we prove
the jump and limit properties of these potentials. (These results were proved for the
usual Stokes operator on Lipschitz domains in [100], but our method is different.)
The proof of the jumpt/limit relations is based on the “algebra tool kit” developed
for this purpose in the fourth and sixth sections of this paper.
In the ninth section, we specialize again to the case of a cylinder M = S×R with
an invariant metric and study the Fourier transforms (or indicial operators) of the
corresponding layer potential operators. In combination with the Green formulas
on cylinders, this allows us to establish the invertibility of the main layer potential
operators S and 1
2 + K on a cylinder. This result is interesting in itself and is then
used to study these operators on a general manifold with straight cylindrical ends.
The tenth section is devoted to Fredholmness and invertibility of the Stokes
layer potential operators when M is a general manifold with straight cylindrical
ends, depending on the properties of our potentials V and V0. Thus, under suitable
positivity assumptions on V and V0, we prove the invertibility of the single layer
potential operator S and of the operator 1
2 + K, where K is the corresponding
double layer potential operator, on Sobolev spaces on the boundary of a domain
with straight cylindrical ends. These are some of the main results of this paper
and rely on the results developed in the previous sections. (The presentation in
this paper is such as to lead as quickly as possible to this invertibility result, while
preserving clarity and completeness, but sacrificing occasionally the generality.)
In the eleventh section, we prove the main well-posedness result of this paper,
that is, the well-posedness of the Dirichlet problem for the generalized Stokes sys-
tem, Theorem 1.1, assuming that Ωis a smooth domain with straight cylindrical
ends in a connected manifold with straight cylindrical ends M and suitable positiv-
ity assumptions on V and V0. Our proof uses layer potentials. This well-posedness
result is useful to define the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator for the generalized
Stokes system and to prove that the conormal derivative of the Stokes double layer
potential has no jump across the boundary of the domain Ω, Theorem 11.6.
The twelfth (and last) section is devoted to applications and extensions. As an
application, we obtain an existence and uniqueness result for the Dirichlet problem
for the generalized Navier-Stokes system (12.4) in a smooth domain Ωwith straight
cylindrical ends assuming k ∈Z+, dim M = n < 2(k + 3), that the given data is in
Hk(Ω; TM)⊕Hk+3/2(∂Ω; TM) and is sufficiently small in a sense that is described
in detail in Theorem 12.3. The proof is based on the well-posedness of the Dirichlet
problem for the non-homogeneous generalized Stokes system, Theorem 11.4, and a
fixed point theorem. In the last subsection of this section (and of this paper), we
state the well-posedness of the Dirichlet problem for the generalized Stokes system
on a closed manifold [69]. We also explain what modifications need to be brought
to the statements of our results for manifolds with straight cylindrical ends so that
they are valid also in the case of closed manifolds. These resuls were proved in [69]
(for closed manifolds), so in this paper we content ourselves with reminding a few
of these results.

1.4. Overview of the existing literature. The layer potentials method is a clas-
sical method for solving elliptic boundary value problems [32, 35, 37, 39, 45, 54,
55, 59, 63, 76, 87, 96, 117, 134]. It plays a crucial role in the analysis of elliptic
boundary value problems in various function spaces, their main advantage consist-
ing in the fact that they provide explicit formulas for the the solutions and that
they provide a convenient way of extending the results to Lp-type Sobolev spaces.
There are also results that can be proved at this time only using layer potential
methods. An example is provided by our well-posedness theorem, Theorem 1.1.
More on the history of the method of layer potentials can be found, for example,
in [2, 43, 45, 54, 59, 76, 87, 115, 135].
Let us now review some of the more specialized results in the subject, most
of them from research papers devoted to the analysis of the Stokes and Navier-
Stokes equations on domains, in Euclidean spaces, or on Riemannian manifolds.
Fabes, Kenig and Verchota [43] initiated the study of boundary value problems
for the Stokes system in Lipschitz domains of Rn with data in L2-based Sobolev
spaces and obtained various mapping properties for the corresponding layer po-
tentials. They also obtained well-posedness results for the Dirichlet and Neumann
problems in Lp-based spaces, with p in a neighborhood of 2, by combining Rellich
type formulas with layer potential approaches. Mitrea and Wright [100] used layer
potential methods and extended the results in [43] to a variety of Lp, Sobolev,
Bessel potential, and Besov spaces. They also obtained well-posedness results for
the main boundary value problems for the constant coefficient Stokes system in
arbitrary Lipschitz domains in Euclidean spaces, together with the determination
of the optimal range for p. The authors of [61] obtained mapping properties for
the constant-coefficient Stokes and Brinkman layer potential operators in standard
and weighted Sobolev spaces on R3. Shen [127] used a layer potential approach and
obtained resolvent estimates in Lp spaces for the Stokes operator in Lipschitz do-
mains in the Euclidean spaces. Layer potential theoretical methods have been also
combined with fixed point theorems in the analysis of boundary value problems for
(linear and nonlinear) elliptic systems with nonlinear boundary conditions. For in-
stance, the authors in [62] used a layer potential approach and the Leray-Schauder
fixed point theorem to show the existence result in Lp, Sobolev, and Besov spaces
for the constant-coefficient Stokes and Brinkman systems with nonlinear Neumann-
transmission conditions. Girault and Sequeira [46] used a variational approach to
prove the well-posedness of the exterior Dirichlet problem for the Stokes system
on exterior Lipschitz domains in Rn, n = 2, 3. Galdi [45, Chapters 5, 11, 12] used
variational approaches in the analysis of steady Stokes and Navier-Stokes flows in
exterior domains. Medkov´a [90] used an integral equation method to analyze the
Dirichlet problem for the Brinkman and Darcy-Forchheimer-Brinkman systems in
Sobolev spaces on unbounded domains with compact Lipschitz boundary in Rn.
Exterior Dirichlet and transmission problems for anisotropic Stokes and Navier-
Stokes systems with L∞variable coefficients have been studied in [65] and [63,
Chapters 5-7] using a variational approach and in [92]. Other boundary problems
for the Stokes system in Sobolev spaces on Lipschitz domains via layer potential
theoretical methods have also been studied in [88, 89, 91, 115, 118, 136].
An important paper is Costabel’s contribution [35] that establishes many very
useful properties of layer potentials using elementary methods. Chandler-Wilde et


## Page 10


10
M. KOHR, V. NISTOR, AND W.L. WENDLAND
al. [31, 32] studied boundary integral equations on locally-dilation-invariant Lips-
chitz domains and fractal screens. Buffa, Hiptmair, Petersdorff, and Schwab [28]
studied Maxwell transmission problems in Lipschitz domains by using boundary
element methods. Labarca-Figueroa and Hiptmair [75] used coupled boundary and
volume integral equations in the study of electromagnetic scattering. Singularly
perturbed boundary value problems based on a functional analytic approach have
been studied by M. Dalla Riva, M. Lanza de Cristoforis, and P. Musolino [37]. This
approach, which also uses a layer potential analysis, is very useful in the study of
various linear nonlinear elliptic problems. A nonvariational form of the acoustic sin-
gle layer potential has been obtained by Lanza de Cristoforis [78]. A nonvariational
form of the Neumann problem for H¨older continuous harmonic functions, based
also on a layer potential analysis, has been obtained in [77]. Ando, Kang, Miyan-
ishi, and Putinar have obtained Carleman factorizations of layer potentials [15].
The recent book [96] of D. Mitrea, I. Mitrea and M. Mitrea is a rigorous interplay
between Harmonic Analysis, Geometric Measure Theory, Function Space Theory,
and Partial Differential Equations, and provide a powerful tool in the analysis of
boundary problems for complex coefficient elliptic systems in various geometric
settings, including the class of Lipschitz domains. The theory of Fredholm and
layer potential operators in Euclidean spaces and in Riemannian manifolds plays
an important role in their book [96] (and also in our book [63]).
The layer potential methods are also very useful in the analysis of elliptic bound-
ary value problems on manifolds. Next we provide a brief overview of some of the
important contributions related especially to the Stokes and Navier-Stokes equa-
tions in the setting of compact or non-compact Riemannian manifolds. Mitrea and
Taylor [99] and Dindo˘s and Mitrea [39] obtained the well-posedness of the Dirich-
let problem for the Stokes and Navier-Stokes systems with smooth coefficients on
Lipschitz domains in closed Riemannian manifolds and in Lp, Sobolev and Besov
spaces, by using the mapping properties of Stokes layer potentials, (see also [69]
for similar results for a modified Stokes operator).
In [64], the authors studied
transmission problems for the smooth coefficients Navier-Stokes type equations in
Lipschitz domains on closed Riemannian manifolds by using a layer potential ap-
proach combined with a fixed point theorem. Mitrea and Monniaux [97] studied
the analyticity of the semigroup generated by the Stokes operator equipped with
Neumann-type boundary conditions on Lp-spaces on Lipschitz domains of closed
Riemannian manifolds. In [70], the authors proved the well-posedness of Dirichlet
problems for the Stokes and Navier-Stokes systems with L∞-variable coefficients
in L2-based Sobolev spaces in Lipschitz domains on closed Riemannian manifolds.
They used a variational approach to define the Newtonian and layer potentials
for the non-smooth coefficients Stokes system on Lipschitz surfaces in closed Rie-
mannian manifolds. Benavides, Nochetto, and Shakipov [18] used a variational ap-
proach and studied the well-posedness and regularity in Lp-based Sobolev spaces,
p ∈(1, ∞), for the weak formulations of the stationary tangent Stokes and tangent
Navier-Stokes systems on a compact and connected d-dimensional manifold without
boundary of class Cm, m ≥2, embedded in Rd+1, in terms of the regularity of the
source terms and the manifold.
The analysis on non-compact manifolds poses more challenges than that the one
on compact manifolds. A significant issue in this sense is the construction of the
classes of pseudodifferential operators. In this regard, we refer to the contributions


## Page 11


WELL-POSEDNESS FOR A GENERALIZED STOKES OPERATOR
11
of Ammann, Lauter and Nistor [11, 12], Mazzeo and Melrose [84], Melrose [93],
Melrose and Mendoza [95], Nistor [103], Nistor, Weinstein, and Xu [104] Schrohe
[120, 121], Schulze [122, 123], Schulze and Wong [124], Shubin [128], to mention
just a few among very many others.
Another important issue in the analysis on non-compact manifolds is finding
the “right definition” of the relevant function spaces. In this paper, as in [66], we
have defined our function spaces by using connections. See also [3, 4, 119]. Amann
[4] and Ammann, Große, Nistor [6, 8] studied function spaces on manifolds with
bounded geometry and obtained well-posedness results for the Laplace operator.
Using the uniform Shapiro-Lopatinski conditions, Große and Nistor [49] obtained
regularity results for elliptic boundary value problems on manifolds with bounded
geometry.
Lie manifolds are special manifolds with bounded geometry [10, 11],
which allow the implementation of the layer potential methods. See also [101, 102].
The layer potential theory for the Laplace operator on manifolds with cylindrical
ends was initiated by Lewis and Parenti [81] and further extended by Mitrea and
Nistor [98]. Manifolds with cylindrical ends are very useful in the study of boundary
value problems on manifolds with conical points.
See, for example, the papers
[10, 27, 33, 38, 47, 71, 72, 73, 79, 82, 85, 94, 124], among many others devoted to
the subject. The reason is provided by the fact that a domain with a conical point
is mapped via the Kondratiev transformation [71] to a manifold with cylindrical
ends. This fact was exploited by Qiao and Nistor [111] to obtain a solvability result
for the Dirichlet problem for the Laplace operator in an open straight cone in Rn,
n ≥3, in the setting of Kondratiev-Babuˇska weighted (Sobolev) spaces. Roidos,
Schrohe and Seiler [113] studied the H∞-calculus in Lp-based Sobolev spaces for
parameter-elliptic boundary value problems on manifolds with conical singularities
at the boundary.
As an application they treated the porous medium equation
on manifolds with conical singularities (see also [112] and the references therein).
Mitrea and Nistor [98] and Kohr, Nistor and Wendland [63, 67, 68] developed
an essentially translation invariant pseudodifferential calculus on manifolds with
cylindrical ends, which is very useful to provide the invertibility and structure of
the Stokes operator and, as a consequence, the construction and the invertibility
the Stokes layer potential operators on manifolds with cylindrical ends. Examples
of domains with conical points are the polygonal domains and some polyhedral
domains.
Manifolds with cylindrical ends are particular cases of Lie manifolds
[11, 29] and of manifolds with bounded geometry [6, 7, 8] (see also the references
therein). The papers by Carvalho and Qiao [30], Qiao and Li [110], Qiao [109],
Putinar and Perfect [105, 106] contain results on layer potential on polyhedral
domains. See also [13, 14].
In [48], Große, Kohr, and Nistor have obtained the L2-unique continuation prop-
erty for the deformation operator Def on manifolds with bounded geometry, a result
that was used for the analysis of the Stokes various classes of manifolds, includ-
ing manifolds with cylindrical ends, in [63, 67, 68].
Manifolds with cylindrical
ends appear in may applications that involve “wave-guides” or “layers” (see e.g.
[19, 20, 21, 22, 23, 24, 25, 42, 63, 68, 74, 137]). Let us finally add that the main
operator in the structure of the Stokes and Navier-Stokes equations on Riemannian
manifolds is the deformation Laplacian L := 2 Def ∗Def . In their groundbreak-
ing paper [40], Ebin and Marsden described the deformation Laplacian as the right


## Page 12


12
M. KOHR, V. NISTOR, AND W.L. WENDLAND
Laplace type operator to describe the Navier-Stokes equation on compact Riemann-
ian manifolds. Pierfelice [108] also used the deformation-Laplacian for the definition
and the analysis of the Navier-Stokes equation on a non-compact Riemannian man-
ifold with negative Ricci curvature. This is the choice that we also adopt in the
description of our Stokes operator Ξ of Equation (1.3). Our generalized Stokes
operator is closely related to the Brinkman operator. The Brinkman flow was stud-
ied by Karageorghis, Lesnic, and Marin in [57, 58], and Bacuta, Hassell, Hsiao,
and Sayas in [26]. A class of solutions to the Navier-Stokes equations for flows in
plane-parallel channels and related singular perturbation problems were studied by
Mazzucato and Taylor [86].

2. Green formulas for the Stokes operator
In this section we introduce the basic differential operators used for the study
of our (generalized) Stokes operators and establish Green formulas and energy es-
timates for the Stokes operator. More precisely, in the first part of this section,
Subsection 2.1, we introduce and study the deformation operator Def , Equation
(2.1). The deformation operator appears in the definition of the the Stokes opera-
tor Ξ = ΞV,V0 introduced in Equation (1.3). We also introduce a few other basic
differential operators needed for the study of the Stokes operator. In the second
subsection, we recall an abstract integration by parts formulas, Proposition 2.9.
The last subsection, Subsection 2.3 establishes the needed Green function formulas
for the Stokes operator, as well as some related energy estimates and representation
formulas.
The material contained in this section is thus mostly background material, thus
we also introduce here other needed concepts or notation, mostly related to the
differential operators appearing in the study of the Stokes operator. The results
of this section, while mostly known, are sometimes hard to find in the literature.
We thus provide here a complete and largely self-contained presentation of these
results (a different approach is contained in Section A.3.3 of our book [63]).
In this section, we assume that (M, g) is a Riemannian manifold, occasionally
with some additional properties.
More precisely, in the third subsection of this
section, we assume that M has bounded geometry. Recall that a manifold with
bounded geometry is one that has positive injectivity radius and all the covariant
derivatives of its curvature are bounded. (See [48] for references and for more on
manifolds with bounded geometry. We note that the results of this section will
be used only when M has straight cylindrical ends, and that the manifolds with
straight cylindrical ends have bounded geometry.)
2.1. Differential operators. We now introduce the basic differential operators
used for the study of our (generalized) Stokes operators. If E →M is a (smooth)
vector bundle, then C∞(M; E) denotes the set of smooth sections of E, whereas
C∞
c (M; E) ⊂C∞(M; E) denotes the subset of compactly supported (smooth) sec-
tions of E.
When necessary, the real vector bundles and their sections will be
complexified. All our vector bundles will be smooth. If E = C, it will be omitted
from the notation, thus C∞(M) denotes the set of smooth functions on M.

2.2. An abstract integration by parts formula. We continue to assume that
(M, g) is a Riemannian manifold. We now recall a general (abstract) integration
by parts formula that will often be used in conjunction with the formulas of the
previous subsection.
Our integration by parts formulas will be used either on the manifold M or on
some open subset Ω⊂M with smooth boundary Γ := ∂Ω.
Assumption 2.7. Let Ω⊂M be an open subset.
Recall that Ωdenotes the
closure of Ωand that Γ denotes the boundary ∂Ω:= Ω∖Ωof Ω. We require in
this paper that Ωbe on one side of its boundary. This means that we require one
of the following two equivalent conditions:
(i) If Ω−:= M ∖Ω, then Γ := ∂Ω= ∂Ω−.
(ii) Ωis the interior of Ω.
This assumption is necessary in order to define a smooth vector field ν that, at
Γ, coincides with the unit outer normal vector to Ω. In the case of integration over
M, integration by parts will be performed for compactly supported functions (or
sections), so there will be no boundary terms. However, when using integration by
parts on the domain Ω, we will obtain boundary terms. It is the purpose of this
subsection to give a general formula for these boundary terms. There is no loss of
generality to assume that Ωis connected
We endow ∂Ωwith the measure dSg induced by the metric on M. This allows us

For completeness, we sketch now a proof. See [63] for more details.
Proof. First, if P = a, an order zero multiplication operator, then the formula is
obviously true. Second, if P = X, a smooth vector field, the claimed formula is
nothing but the divergence formula (Remark (2.8)(ii)), which is well known. One
checks easily that if the formula of the proposition is true for P and a and b are
suitable endomorphisms, then the formula is true also for aPb. Since the formula is
linear and all first order differential operators can be written as linear combinations
of differential operators of the form aXb and a, with X a smooth vector field, the
result follows.
□
2.3. Green formulas and energy estimates for the Stokes operator. In this
subsection, (M, g) is a Riemannian manifold with bounded geometry, that is, we
assume that it has positive injectivity radius and all covariant derivatives of the
curvature are bounded. (See [48] for more on manifolds with bounded geomery,
including references.) Also, E →M is a Hermitian vector bundle with connection,
also assume to have bounded geometry, meaning that all the covariant derivatives
of its curvature are bounded. We will let Ω⊂M be an open subset with boundary
Γ := ∂Ω, assumed to be a submanifold with bounded geometry of M (see [9, 63]
definitions). In this paper, we are interested in the case when M is a manifold
with straight cylindrical ends (Definition 3.11), E →M a compatible Hermitian
vector bundle with metric preserving connection (Definition 3.13), and Ω⊂M
also compatible with the straight cylindrical ends structure of M (Equation (1.2)).
Then M, E, and Ωfit into the framework of this subsection (that is M, E, and

We will need the following definition from [48].
Definition 2.12. Let M be a manifold and E and F be two vector bundles over M.
If M is connected, we say that a differential operator T : L2(M; E) →C∞
c (M; F)′
satisfies the L2-unique continuation property if, given u ∈L2(M; E) that vanishes
in a non-empty open subset of M and satisfies Tu = 0, then u = 0 everywhere on
M. For general M, we say that T satisfies the L2-unique continuation property if
it satisfies this property on any connected component of M.
Clearly, an operator that is injective satisfies the L2-unique continuation prop-
erty. The concept of L2-unique continuation property just recalled allows us to
obtain the following corollary. Recall that a Killing vector field X is a vector field
that preserves the metric, equivalently, Def X = 0. We shall need the following

3. General background material and manifolds with cylindrical ends
This section contains some preliminary material on pseudodifferential operators,
normal lateral limits, Fourier transforms and their relation to normal lateral limits,
and manifolds with straight cylindrical ends (definition and vector bundles and
Sobolev spaces on these manifolds). Several related results are contained in the
book [63], to which we refer for further details as well as for the concepts not
defined here. See also [52, 53, 130] for related background material. Except the
last subsection, where we introduce manifolds with straight cylindrical ends, in the
other three subsections we work on Rn.
3.1. Basic function spaces and some Fourier transforms. We now recall
some basic function spaes, the Fourier transform, some other basic concepts, and
compute some Fourier transforms that will be needed in what follows, especially
for the limit and jump relations.

3.4. Manifolds with cylindrical ends, compatible vector bundles and dif-
ferential operators. We continue with some standard background material, but
now we turn from the case of Rn to that of a manifold with cylindrical ends M.
We begin by recalling a definition of manifolds with straight cylindrical ends that
is equivalent to the one given in the introduction (see [63, 67, 68]).
Definition 3.11. A manifold with straight cylindrical ends M is a complete non-
compact Riemannian manifold that is the disjoint union
M = M0 ∪[M ′ × (−∞, RM)] ,
where (M0, g0) is a compact Riemannian manifold, M ′ := ∂M0 ̸= ∅, RM ∈R,
and the cylindrical end M ′ × (−∞, RM) is endowed with a product metric. Any
Riemannian manifold isometrically diffeomorphic to one of this form will also be
called a manifold with straight cylindrical ends.
There is no loss of generality to assume that M is connected, so we will do that
throughout the paper, in order to simplify our statements. It follows from the fact
that M is complete that the isometric embedding M ′×(−∞, 0) ,→M extends to an
isometry M ′ × (−∞, 0] ,→M that maps M ′ × {0} to M ′ ≃∂M0 diffeomorphically.
There is no loss of generality to assume that this diffeomorphism is the canonical
one identifying (x′, 0) ∈M ′ × {0} to x′ ∈M ′ ≃∂M0. So, again, we will assume
that this is the case throughout the paper. Also, there is no loss of generality to
assume RE = 0, and, again, we will usually (but not always) do that. For instance,
for a cylinder, it is convenient to take RE = −1. The case of a cylinder is a very
important one, so we discuss it more formally in the following remark.
Remark 3.12. Recall that a closed manifold is a smooth, compact manifold without
boundary. Let S be a closed manifold. An important example of a manifold with
straight cylindrical ends is M = S × R. In this case, M0 = S × [−1, 1], RM = −1
and M ′ := ∂M0 = S × {−1, 1}. Then we have a canonical isometry
S × R =: M ≃M0 ∪ψ

S × {−1, 1} × (−∞, −1]

,
where, on the right hand side ψ : S × {−1, 1} × {−1} →S × {−1, 1} := ∂M0 is
given by ψ(x′, −1) = x′, for all x′ ∈S × {−1, 1}. (The symbol ∪ψ means that we
identify y to ψ(y) in the union.)
On cylinders, it is important to notice that M := S × R carries a natural action
of R by translations and we let C∞(M)R = C∞(S×R)R ≃C∞(S) denote the space
of smooth functions on S × R that are invariant by translations. Many objects


## Page 31


WELL-POSEDNESS FOR A GENERALIZED STOKES OPERATOR
31
on manifolds with cylindrical ends are defined by first defining their translation
invariant versions on cylinders. If M = M0 ∪

M ′ × (−∞, 0)

is a general manifold
with cylindrical ends, then M ′ × (−∞, 0] is its end and M ′ × R is its associated
cylinder with the induced Riemannian metric (induced from ∂M0, which, in turn,
is induced from the metric on M).
In the following, whenever we will write M = M0 ∪[M ′ × (−∞, 0)], we will
understand that M is a manifold with straight cylindrical ends as in Definition
3.11. It is easy (and well-known) to prove (see e.g. [48] and the references therein)
that all manifolds with straight cylindrical ends have bounded geometry, so the
results of Subsection 2.3 apply to them.
Definition 3.13. Let M = M0 ∪[M ′ × (−∞, 0)] be a manifold with straight cylin-
drical ends (Definition 3.11) and let E →M be a Hermitian vector bundle endowed
with a metric preserving connection ∇E. We shall say that the pair (E, ∇E) is
compatible with the straight cylindrical ends structure on M (simply, compatible)
if there exists RE ≤0 such that E|M ′×(−∞,RE) is isometric to the pull-back to
M ′ × (−∞, RE) of a Hermitian vector bundle E′ →M ′ with connection ∇E′ via
an isomorphism that maps the connection ∇E of E to the pull-back connection of
∇E′ on M ′ × (−∞, RE).
If E = C (the one dimensional trivial bundle), then ∇Ef := df, where d is the de
Rham differential. Let ♯: T ∗M →TM be the vector bundle isomorphism defined
by the Riemannian metric g of M. Note then that the gradient ∇f := ♯df has a
similar notation to a connection, however the two should not be confused.
Let E be a vector bundle on M ′×R obtained from the pull-back of vector bundle
on M ′. Thus E →M ′ × R is, in, in particular, a compatible vector bunde on the
manifold with straight cylindrical ends M ′ × R. We let C∞(M ′ × R)R denote the
set of translation invariant sections of E →M ′ × R. In the rest of this subsection,
M will be a manifold with straight cylindrical ends. (The results are true also for
closed manifolds, see [69] and some of them are recalled in Subsection 12.2.) We
next introduce an important class of smooth sections.
Definition 3.14. Let E →M be a compatible vector bundle and let u ∈C∞(M; E).
We shall say that u is translation invariant in a neighborhood of infinity if there ex-
ists Ru ≤RE such that u(x′, t) = u(x′, s) for all s, t ≤Ru and all x′ ∈M ′ := ∂M0.
We let C∞
inv(M; E) denote the set of sections of E that are translation invariant in a
neighborhood of infinity and let C∞(M ′ ×R; E)R be the set of translation invariant
sections on M ′ × R. Then we let R∞: C∞
inv(M; E) →C∞(M ′ × R; E)R be defined
by
R∞(u)(x′, t) = u(x′, t) ,
for t ≤Ru .
Remark 3.15. If u is as in Definition 3.14, then there exists u0 ∈C∞(M ′; E) such

under the natural isomorphism C∞(M ′ ×R; E)R ≃C∞(M ′; E). Because of this, we
will sometimes regard R∞(u) as an element of C∞(M ′; E).

4. Normal lateral limits of pseudodifferential operators on Rn
In this section we provide a detailed study on Rn of the normal lateral limits
[a(x, D)u]± ∈Hs(Γ)
at Γ := {xn = 0} for u = h ⊗δΓ. Here a(x, D) is a pseudodifferential operator
(Definition 3.5), the normal lateral limits were defined in Definition 3.9, and u =


## Page 33


WELL-POSEDNESS FOR A GENERALIZED STOKES OPERATOR
33
h⊗δΓ was defined in Lemma 3.8. The hyperplane Γ := {xn = 0} separates the two
half-spaces
Ω+ := Rn
+ := {x ∈Rn | xn > 0} and Ω−:= Rn
−:= {x ∈Rn | xn < 0} .
(We remark in passing, that the open set Ω+ := Rn
+ is on one side of its boundary, as
required throughout this paper.) Recall that if a(x, D)u is smooth enough on either
of the half-spaces Rn
±, then the corresponding normal lateral limit [a(x, D)u]± at
Γ = ∂Ω± coincides with the trace γ±a(x, D)u of a(x, D)u at the boundary Γ of
that half-space (see Lemma 3.10).
We have included in the previous section and in this section a few very basic
definition related to pseudodifferential operators. If needed, results not recalled or
proved here about pseudodifferential operators can be found in one of the following
books [1, 51, 52, 116, 132, 139], as well as in may others. For instance, the book [131]
contains a clear but concise account of pseudodifferential operators and applications
to layer potentials. An even shorter introduction to some basic facts and definitions
related to pseudodifferential operators and geared towards our applications can be
found in [67].
A complete and general treatment of normal lateral limits distributions of the
form [a(x, D)h ⊗δΓ]± for pseudodifferential operators a(x, D) is contained in the
book by Hsiao and Wendland [54]. Here we only deal with the results (and calcu-
lations) needed to treat suitable elliptic, second order differential operators. Our
presentation in this section provide a complete, yet concise, introduction to the
subject of limit and jump relations for potential operators on a half-space. These
results, together with those in Section 6 and in [63] provide a short–but complete–
introduction to jump relations on manifolds with cylindrical ends. (They also shed
light on jump relations on general manifolds.)
The main question discussed in this section is the following.
Problem 4.1. Given a ∈Sm(Rn × Rn), to study the existence and properties of
the normal lateral limits (see Definition (3.9)):
a±(x′, D′)h :=

a(x, D)(h ⊗δΓ)

± ,
in particular, to study their relation to the traces γ±a(x, D)(h ⊗δΓ).
Often in the literature, the non-tangential limits at the boundary are studied
instead of the normal lateral limits that we study in this paper. The non-tangential
limits are a stronger form of boundary limit and hence, they exist less often than
the normal lateral limits, however, for functions that are smooth enough, they
are the same. (In fact, they are the same for most of the applications studied in
this paper. We stress, however, that proving the existence of non-tangential limits
is more difficult than the proof of the existence of the normal lateral limits. In
particular, the normal lateral limits may exist even if the non-tangential limits do
not exist.)
A word now about the notation. First, we often parametrize Γ := {xn = 0} with
Rn−1. Thus Γ ⊂Rn, but Γ ≃Rn−1 ̸⊂Rn. This is the reason we need the isometries
τt, τt(x) := x −ten mapping Γt := Rn−1 × {t} to Γ (see Equation (3.18) and the
discussion following it). We distinguish Γ from Rn−1 to make it easier to transition
in the later sections to an arbitrary domain (i.e., and open connected subset) with
boundary Γ. However, in this paper, there will be no situation when confusions

We are now ready to formulate our main result concerning the limit/jump values
of classical matrix valued pseudodifferential operators of order < −1. So far, we
have formulated and proved our results for scalar symbols. The proofs extend,
however, immediately to the vector valued case. We thus formulate the following
results for sections of a trivial k-dimensional vector bundle.

Remark 4.14. It is known that the distribution kernel of a negative order opera-
tor cannot be supported on the diagonal (because the pseudodifferential operators
supported on the diagonal are local, and hence they are multiplication operators,
by Peetre’s theorem [54, 52, 53], that is, order zero operators).
Therefore, the


## Page 41


WELL-POSEDNESS FOR A GENERALIZED STOKES OPERATOR
41
restriction of the distribution kernel of a negative order operator outside the di-
agonal completely determines the operator (this is relevant in view of Theorems
4.13(v) and 4.16(i) and (ii), for whose proof, a similar argument was used). Thus,
if m < −1, the knowledge of the distribution kernel ka(x,D) of a pseudodifferential
operator a(x, D) of order m completely determines the distribution kernel ka0(x′,D′)
and, hence, it completely determines a0 and a0(x′, D′).

5. Pseudodifferential operators on manifolds with straight
cylindrical ends
From now on, M will be a manifold with straight cylindrical ends, M = M0 ∪

M ′×(−∞, 0)

. Also, in the following, E →M will be a fixed compatible Hermitian
vector bundle. We now define two needed classes of pseudodifferential operators on
M, the “inv-calculus” and the “ess-calculus.” For M closed, these definitions are
trivial (they recover the usual pseudodifferential operators, so the give nothing
new), so the only interesting case is the one when M is not compact. Including
the case M compact is useful, however, in order to formulate our results in a more
general setting, as they are true for both closed manifolds and for manifolds with
straight cylindrical ends.
The resulting classes of pseudodifferential operators are very closely related to
the b- and c- calculi of Melrose [93, 84]. Related calculi have also been considered by
Schulze and Schrohe [121, 122, 124]. See [67] for more references and for a discussion
of the relation between our calculi and the ones of Mazzeo, Melrose, Schrohe, and
Schulze. The results of this section are close to those in [63, 67]. The presentation is,
however, different and, occasionally, it provides more general results. For instance,
in [63], we work only with classical pseudodifferential operators, whereas here we
allow the Kohn-Nirenberg classes of symbols recalled in Definition 3.5.
5.1. Translation invariance in a neighborhood at infinity: inv-calculus.

5.5. Limit operators. An important concept associated to the operators in the
b-calculus is that of “normal operator” (in Melrose’s terminology) or “conormal
principal symbol” (in Schulze’s terminology, who uses the term “cone-calculus”
instead of the b-calculus). This concept is specific to non-compact manifolds and
is easy to be defined for the inv-calculus. We shall call these operators “limit at
infinity operators,” (or “limit operators,” for short). None of the results of this
section have analogues for closed manifolds.
The translation invariant operators appear in the following lemma that will pro-
vide the definition of limit operators. The proof of this lemma can be found in [67]
(see also [63], [68]).

6.2. Normal lateral limits for manifolds with cylindrical ends. We now
turn to the study of normal lateral limits of pseudodifferential operators on general
Riemannian manifolds with straight cylindrical ends. Note that, unlike in the other

review it in detail here.)
As usual, the case of pseudodifferential operators of order m < −1 is easier. We
begin with the case of operators with compactly supported distribution kernels in

6.3. Douglis-Nirenberg operators: jumps, ellipticity, and Fredholmness.
The generalized Stokes operator Ξ := ΞV,V0 (Equation 1.3) is not elliptic in the
usual sense, but is elliptic in Douglis-Nirenberg sense. As with the usual ellipticity
property, the ellipticity in Douglis-Nirenberg sense (together with the invertibility of
the limit operators) is enough to establish the Fredholm property of our generalized
Stokes operator Ξ := ΞV,V0, but under different Sobolev spaces. This is the content
of Theorem 6.15. The definition of Douglis −Nirenberg-operators makes sense for
general manifolds M, so, in the beginning of this subsection, we allow M to be a
general smooth manifold.
Definition 6.11. Let M be a smooth manifold, let Ei →M smooth vector bundles,
1 ≤i ≤k, and let si, tj ∈R, 1 ≤i, j ≤k. We set s := (s1, . . . , sk), t := (t1, . . . , tk),
and E := E1 ⊕. . . ⊕Ek. Then
Ψ[s+t](M; E) := {P = [Pij] | Pij ∈Ψsi+tj(M; Ej, Ei), 1 ≤i, j ≤k} .
An operator P = [Pij] ∈Ψ[s+t](M; TM⊕C) is said to be of Douglis-Nirenberg-order
≤[s + t]. For P = [Pij] ∈Ψ[s+t](M; TM ⊕C) let
Symbs,t(P) := [σsi+tj(Pij)]
be its (s, t)–principal symbol, which is a suitable section of the lift of the endomor-
phism bundle End(E) to T ∗M ∖0. The operator P is said to be (s, t)–Douglis-
Nirenberg elliptic if its (s, t)–principal symbol matrix Symbs,t(P) is invertible out-
side the zero section (i.e., on T ∗M ∖0).
The reason for introducing the spaces Ψ[s+t](M; E) is that our generalized Stokes
operator fits into this framework.
Example 6.12. Let k = 2, let s = (s1, s2) = t = (t1, t2) = (1, 0), let E1 = TM,
and let E2 = C. Then
Ξ := ΞV,V0 ∈Ψ[s+t](M; TM ⊕C) := {P = [Pij] | Pij ∈Ψsi+tj(M; Ej, Ei)} .
More importantly, Ξ is not elliptic in the usual sense, but we will see in Proposition
6.13 that Ξ is (s, t)-Douglis-Nirenberg-elliptic.
Recall that ♯: T ∗M →TM denotes the isomorphism defined by the Riemannian
metric g of M (it is sometimes called the “musical isomorphism”). For instance,
using the notation introduced in Notation (2.3), if 0 ̸= η ∈TxM, then

7. Green formulas and invertibility of Ξ on cylinders
We now extend the Green formulas and the results in Section 2 to the case of
a cylinder. We then use these results to prove the invertibility of our generalized
Stokes operator Ξ := ΞV,V0 (Equation (1.3)) on a cylinder.
We thus assume in this section that M := S × R, where S is a closed manifold
(i.e., smooth, compact, without boundary). Moreover, all other objects used in this
section will be translation invariant, thus Ω= Ω′ × R and V and V0 descend to
functions on S, denoted, respectively, bV and bV0 (see Equation (5.6) for a justifica-
tion of this notation). Explicitly, we have V (x′, t) = bV (x′) and V0(x′, t) = bV0(x′)
for x′ ∈S and t ∈R. The results of this section will be used for S possibly dis-
connected, so we will not assume that S is connected, although the proofs of the
results in this section can be reduced immediately to the connected case. Note also
that we are continuing to assume that M is a manifold with cylindrical ends, albeit
of a very particular kind. Recall that the scalar product on L2(M; E) is
(f, g)Ω:=

is that our scalar products are conjugate linear in the second variable and linear in
the first.

study the associated indicial operators and prove that they have the L2-unique
continuation property.

7.3. The invertibility of Ξ on a cylinder. Recall from Theorem 6.15 that the
Fredholm property of an operator depends on the invertibility of its limit operator,
which acts on the cylinder associated to M. The limit operator of ΞV,V0 is ΞbV ,bV0.
It is the purpose of this subsection to establish the invertibility of ΞbV ,bV0 acting on
the associated cylinder under the assumption that bV ≻0 and bV0 ≻0 and bV0 ≻0
on S.
We thus continue to assume, in this subsection, that M is the cylinder M =
S × R with S a closed manifold and with a product metric.
We also assume
that V (x′, t) = bV (x′) and that V0(x′, t) = bV0(x′), and hence V and V0 are thus
translation invariant. We also assume that they are both ≻0 on M (see Definition
2.13). This condition is equivalent to bV ≻0 and bV0 ≻0 on S.
Let us notice that, since M := S × R is endowed with the product metric,
this metric is translation invariant, and hence Ξ is also translation invariant. We
can then define its Fourier transform (or indicial family) bΞ(τ) as in Section 5.9,
especially Definition 5.22 (and the discussion surrounding it). In particular, we
choose the definition that avoids duplications, see Remark also 5.28.
Proposition 7.11. Let us assume that M = S × R is a cylinder and that bV ≻0
and bV0 ≻0 on S. Let s = t = (1, 0). Then bΞ(0) := bΞV,V0(0) is invertible and
bΞ(0)−1 ∈Ψ[−s−t](S; TM ⊕C).
Proof. The generalized Stokes operator bΞ(0) is (s, t)–Douglis-Nirenberg elliptic be-
cause Ξ is (s, t)–Douglis-Nirenberg elliptic (Proposition 6.13) and because ellipticity
is preserved by taking indicial families. Because S is compact, Theorem 6.15 then
gives that bΞ(0) is Fredholm as an operator
bΞ(0) : H1(S; TM) ⊕L2(S) →H−1(S; TM) ⊕L2(S) .
The second Green formula of Proposition 7.5 shows that bΞ(0) is self-adjoint, and
hence it has index zero. Therefore, in order to show that bΞ(0) induces a bijection
between the above spaces, it suffices to show that it is injective. Let then U =

u
p

= ( u p )⊤∈L2(S; TM⊕C) be such that bΞ(0)U = 0. The ellipticity of bΞ(0)
guarantees then that U ∈H∞(S; TM ⊕C), by elliptic regularity (see, for example,
Theorem 15.3.30 in [63]). Assumption 8.3 allow us to use Corollary 7.8 to conclude
that U = 0. Therefore bΞ(0) is invertible as an operator between the indicated
spaces. The property that its inverse belongs to the space Ψ[−s−t](S; TM ⊕C)
follows from the work of Beals and others (see Theorem 7.4 in [67] for more details).
□
The case τ ̸= 0 is completely similar, but only requires the weaker assumptions
bV := R∞(V ) ≥0 and bV0 := R∞(V0) ≥0.
Proposition 7.12. Let us assume that M = S × R is a cylinder, that τ ̸= 0,
and that bV0, bV ≥0. Let s = t = (1, 0). Then bΞ(τ) is invertible and bΞ(τ)−1 ∈
Ψ[−s−t](S; TM ⊕C).
Proof. The proof is almost the same as that of Proposition 7.11 (but easier). The
only difference is that, instead of Corollary 7.8, we use Corollary 7.10, which only
requires that bV and bV0 be non-negative.
□

Standard properties of pseudodifferential operators yield the following result,
which will, however, not be used in this paper. It does specify, however, in what
sense we are talking about the invertibility of our operators bΞ(τ).
Remark 7.13. Let us assume that M = S × R with S compact and that bV and bV0
are non-negative. Then, for any τ, r ∈R, τ ̸= 0, we have an isomorphism
bΞ(τ) := bΞV,V0(τ) : Hr+1(S; TM) ⊕Hr(S) →Hr−1(S; TM) ⊕Hr(S) .
The result is true also for τ = 0 if we further assume that M, V , and V0 satisfy
the non-vanishing Assumption 8.3. Under the same conditions, we have bΞ(τ)−1 ∈
Ψ[−s−t](S; TM ⊕C).
The last two propositions then give the invertibility of Ξ on cylinders (provided
that the positivity at infinity assumption 8.1 is satisfied).
Theorem 7.14. Let us assume that M = S × R, that V and V0 are translation
invariant and that bV ≻0 and bV0 ≻0 on S. Then the operator
Ξ := ΞV,V0 : H1(M; TM) ⊕L2(M) →H−1(M; TM) ⊕L2(M)
is invertible. If s = t = (1, 0), its inverse satisfies Ξ−1 ∈Ψ[−s−t]
ess
(M; TM ⊕C) and
maps Hr+1(M; TM) ⊕Hr(M) →Hr−1(M; TM) ⊕Hr(M) isomorphically for all
r ∈R.
Proof. We know from Propositions 7.11 and 7.12 that bΞ(τ) is invertible for all
τ ∈R. It is well-known from the works of Melrose, Schulze and others (see also
Theorem 5.11 in [68]) that then Ξ is invertible as stated. Once we have proved
that Ξ is invertible, the fact that Ξ−1 ∈Ψ[−s−t]
ess
(M; TM ⊕C) follows from the
work of Beals and others (see Theorem 7.4 in [67] for further details). The claimed
isomorphisms are obtained from the mapping properties of translation invariant
operators.
□
8. Invertibility of Ξ, layer potentials, and jump relations on
manifolds with cylindrical ends
We now use the results of the previous sections and prove the invertibility of
several Stokes-type operators (under suitable positivity conditions on the potentials
V and V0) and use these results to define our layer potentials. We then prove the
main jump formulas for these layer potentials.
We assume from now on that M = M0 ∪

M ′ × (−∞, 0)

is a manifold with
straight cylindrical ends, so M ′ is a closed manifold (i.e., compact, boundaryless).
The case of closed manifolds was treated in [69] and will be briefly reminded in
Subsection 12.2. Also, from now on, we shall assume that V and V0 are translation
invariant at infinity and that they are are smooth. (Some more precise assumption
will be formulated later, see Assumptions 8.1 and 8.3.)
8.1. The Fredholm and invertibility properties of Ξ. Using the results of the
previous subsection, we now recall the proof of the Fredholm property, respectively,
of the invertibility property of Ξ := ΞV,V0 under suitable positivity assumptions
on V and V0 (Assumptions 8.1 and, respectively, 8.3).
These results were first
established in [68]. The proof relies on the “Mitrea-Taylor” trick [99] and on the
results and methods from [67]. To establish the Fredholm property, we shall need
the following assumption (slightly stronger than Assumption ).

The invertibility of Ξ := ΞV,V0 in the case M closed is obtained when both
V and V0 do not vanish identically on M. Moreover, the condition that V does
not vanish identically on M can be replaced with the condition that M does not
have non-vanishing Killing vector fields. These results were proved in [69, Theorem
6.1(4)]. In relation to this result, the reader might have noticed that we are not
considering the case when M = M0 ∪

M ′ × (−∞, 0)

has straight cylindrical ends
but does not have non-vanishing Killing vector fields. In fact, this condition is not
very useful for manifolds with straight cylindrical ends because, even if M does
not have non-vanishing Killing vector fields, its “end” M′ × R will always have
non-vanishing Killing vector fields, for instance ∂t (the infinitesimal generator of
the translations along R). (See Subsection 12.2 for more on the case M closed.)

continue to assume that M has straight cylindrical ends. We also assume that V

9. Calculations on a cylinder
We shall need definitions and results for the limit operators R∞(Ξ) that are
analogous to those in the previous two sections. To this end, in this section we
consider the particular case when our manifold M with straight cylindrical ends is
actually a cylinder M = S × R with a product metric. We do not assume S to be
connected, though. Then, as in Section 7, we let Ω= Ω′ × R ⊂M be a product
type open subset, and hence with straight cylindrical ends, where Ω′ ⊂S. Let
Γ′ := ∂Ω′. In this section, in addition to V and V ≥0 smooth, we assume that
they are translation invariant, and hence V (x′, t) = bV (x′) and V0(x′, t) = bV0(x′)
identify to sections on S. Also, in this section, we use Assumption 8.1, that is that
bV ≻0 and that bV0 ≻0 on S (that is, they have points where they are strictly
positive in every connected component of the space of definition, see Definition
2.13). Also, recall that Ωis on one side of its boundary (Assumption 2.7), and
hence Ω′ is also on one side of its boundary.

9.2. Invertibility of S on a cylinder. We keep the assumptions of the previous
subsection. That is,
• M = S × R,
• V and V ≥0 are smooth, translation invariant, and hence V (x′, t) = bV (x′)
and V0(x′, t) = bV0(x′) identify to sections on S.
• V and V0 satisfy the positivity assumption at infinity (Assumption 8.1, that
is, bV ≻0 and bV0 ≻0).
(Recall that ≻was introduced in Definition 2.13.) We also keep the notation and
assumptions of equations (9.2), (9.1), and (9.3).
From now on, all layer potential operators S, K, ... (possibly decorated with
indices) are associated to the boundaries Γ and Γ′ of the open sets Ω=: Ω+ and
Ω′ =: Ω′
+, see Definitions 8.7, 8.7, and 9.2.
We now prove the invertibility of the single layer potential operator S := A0,
see Theorem 8.16 on our cylinder M = S × R, if V and V0 satisfy the positivity
assumption at infinity (Assumption 8.1). In order to do that, we need to first study
its indicial operators bS (τ), τ ∈R. We begin with the case τ = 0, which requires
more assumptions than the case τ ̸= 0.
Proposition 9.7. Let us assume that M = S × R (is a cylinder), that V and V0
are smooth, translation invariant at infinity, that Ω= Ω′ × R, and that Ω′ ⊂S is
on one side of its boundary Γ′ := ∂Ω′ (i.e., Ω′ is the interior of Ω′′ ⊂S). We also
assume that
(i) bV ≻0 on S and
(ii) bV0 ≻0 on S ∖Γ′.
Let bS (0) ∈Ψ−1(Γ′; TM) be the indicial operator of the operator S := A0 ∈
Ψ−1(Γ; TM) of Theorem 8.16. Then bS (0) is invertible and bS (0)−1 ∈Ψ1(Γ′; TM).
Proof. Our assumptions imply that V and V0 satisfy the non-vanishing conditions
of Assumption 8.3 on M = S × R, and hence the layer potential operator S :=
A0 ∈Ψ−1
ess(Γ; TM) is defined (see Proposition 8.5). We know from Proposition 9.6
that bS (0) is elliptic with self-adjoint principal symbol. Because S is compact, bS (0)
is then Fredholm of index zero, by classical results (the “classical case” of a closed
manifold in Theorem 6.15).
Let us show now that ker bS (0) = 0. Let then h′ ∈L2(Γ′; TM) be such that
bS (0)h′ = 0. Our assumptions imply that bΞ(0) is invertible (Proposition 7.11),
and hence the indicial single layer potential U = ( u p )⊤:= S0(h′) is defined (see
Definition 9.2). Since bS (0) is elliptic and Γ′ is smooth and compact, we have that
h′ ∈Hs(Γ′; TM) for all s ∈R, by elliptic regularity. Proposition 9.5 for s = 1/2
then gives that the restrictions of U to Ω′
+ := Ω′ and to Ω′
−:= S ∖Ω′ satisfy
(9.4)
U|Ω′
± := ( u p )⊤|Ω′
± := S0(h′)|Ω′
± ∈H2(Ω′
±; TM) ⊕H1(Ω′
±) .

then that u+ = u−= bS (0)h′ = 0. Therefore u = 0 in Ω′
−and in Ω′
+, by Corollary
7.7. The same corollary gives that p is constant on each connected component of
S ∖Γ′. Because bV0 is not identically equal to zero on any connected component
of S ∖Γ′, we obtain that, in fact, p = 0. We have thus obtained that U = 0 and
hence bS (0) is injective.

11. The Dirichlet problem for the generalized Stokes system via
layer potentials
In this section, we present two applications of the results of the previous section.
First, we obtain the well-posedness result of the Dirichlet problem for the gener-
alized Stokes system on a smooth domain Ωwith straight cylindrical ends. Then,
we use this well-posedness result to show the no-jump property of the co-normal
derivative T νDST(h) across the boundary of the domain Ω.
11.1. The homogeneous Stokes system. The invertibility of each of the opera-
tors 1
2 + K and S gives the following well-posedness result for the modified Stokes
system with Dirichlet boundary condition, problem (1.4). This result is known for
the usual Stokes operator (i.e. when V = 0 and V0 = 0, see [100, Proposition 10.5.1,
Theorem 10.6.2] in the case of a bounded Lipschitz domain in Rn, n ≥2, and [39,
Theorem 5.1] in the case of a C1 domain on a compact manifold, see also [63] for
further related applications). We consider the homogeneous Dirichlet problem
ΞU := ΞV,V0U = 0 in Ω

12. Applications and extensions
We now consider an application and an extension of our results so far.
12.1. The Dirichlet problem for the generalized Navier-Stokes system.
The first part is of this section is devoted to the Dirichlet problem for a Navier-
Stokes type system on a manifold with cylindrical ends. We analyze the Dirichlet
problem when the norms of the given data (the right hand side of the Navier-Stokes
equation and the Dirichlet datum) are restricted to satisfy a boundedness condition
which ensures the well-posedness of the Dirichlet problem.
A key role in the analysis of the nonlinear Dirichlet problem for the Navier-
Stokes system on a domain with cylindrical ends is played by estimates of the
product of two functions in suitable Sobolev spaces. An example is the following
result, which is well known in Rn (see e.g. [17, Theorem 7.3, Theorem 8.2], [114,
§4.5.2, Theorem], and [90, Lemma 8.1]).
Lemma 12.1. Let m ∈Z+ and M be as in Assumption 8.3. Assume that 2 ≤
dim M = n < 2(m + 2). Then there exists Cm > 0 with the following property: for
all u ∈Hm+1(M) and v ∈Hm(M), we have uv ∈Hm−1(M) and
∥uv∥Hm−1(M) ≤Cm∥u∥Hm+1(M)∥v∥Hm(M) .
Proof. This follows from the analogous result on Rn just mentioned above using a
dyadic partition of unity (see, for instance [63]).
□
We notice that the same argument works for manifolds with bounded geometry.
Also, notice that the result of Lemma 12.1 extends to Sobolev spaces on sections of
vector bundles over M (see e.g. [114, §4.2.2, Proposition] in the Euclidean setting),
and the next lemma is a consequence of this result (see [39, Lemma 7.5] in the case
of a Lipschitz domain on a compact manifold).
Lemma 12.2. Let M be as in Assumption 8.3 and Ω⊂M be as in Theorem
1.1, that is, Ωis a smooth domain with straight cylindrical ends in the connected
manifold with straight cylindrical ends M. Assume that m ∈Z+ and that dim M =
n < 2(m + 2). Then there exists a constant C > 0, such that
∥∇uv∥Hm−1(Ω;T M) ≤C∥u∥Hm+1(Ω;T M)∥v∥Hm+1(Ω;T M),
(12.1)
∥∇uu −∇vv∥Hm−1(Ω;T M)
(12.2)
≤C



for all u, v ∈Hm+1(Ω; TM).

Proof. First, we notice that Ωis a manifold with bounded geometry. Indeed, Ω
is a smooth domain with straight cylindrical ends in the smooth manifold with
straight cylindrical ends M, and hence both M and Ωhave bounded geometry.
Hence the result of Lemma 12.1 holds also on Ω. Thus, for each m ∈Z+ such
that n < 2(m + 2), there exists a constant cm > 0 (depending only on n and the
bounded geometry of M) such that the estimate (12.1) holds for all u ∈Hm+1(Ω)
and v ∈Hm(Ω). Thus, we have uv ∈Hm−1(Ω) and
∥uv∥Hm−1(Ω) ≤cm∥u∥Hm+1(Ω)∥v∥Hm(Ω) .
(see [114, §4.2.2, Proposition] in Rn). This result extends also to vector bundles
and, thus, there exists a constant C > 0 (depending only on n, m and the bounded
geometry of M) such that the estimate (12.1) holds for all u, v ∈Hm+1(Ω; TM).
Moreover, the linearity of the covariant derivative ∇uv with respect to u and
the additivity with respect to v, and estimate (12.1) give
∥∇uu −∇vv∥Hm−1(Ω;T M) ≤∥∇u−vu∥Hm−1(Ω;T M) + ∥∇v(u −v)∥Hm−1(Ω;T M)
≤C


∥u −v∥Hm+1(Ω;T M)
for all u, v ∈Hm+1(Ω; TM), that is, the estimate (12.2).
□

12.2. The case M closed. We now consider the extension of our results to the
case of a closed manifold (unlike most of the other sections of this paper, in which
we considered, for the most part, manifolds with straight cylindrical ends). Recall
that a closed manifold is one that is smooth, compact and does not have a boundary.
Almost all the results on manifolds with straight cylindrical ends hold also for closed
manifolds (when they make sense).
Here are the main modifications:
(1) There are no restrictions “or conditions” at infinity on our operators, func-
tions, and vector bundles. For instance, C∞
inv is replaced with C∞, all open
subsets and all vector bundles are compatible, and Ψm
ess is replaced with
Ψm.
(2) The restuls about the limit at infinity R∞do not make sense anymore (and
are not needed). They have to be ignored in the closed case.
(3) The same is true for the conditions involving R∞; they are to be removed,
as they are no longer needed.
The case M closed was considered in great detail in [69], so we will not go through
the exercice of performing all the modifications abour. Instead, we recall here only
two results from that paper. The first result describes the kernel of Ξ and does not
follow from the results of this paper (directly):
Theorem 12.4. Let us assume that M is a closed manifold, that V, V0 are smooth
and non-negative and that M is a smooth, compact manifold without boundary (i.e.,
a closed manifold). Then Ξ := ΞV,V0 : H1(M; TM) ⊕L2(M) →H−1(M; TM) ⊕
L2(M) is a self-adjoint Fredholm operator. Let N ⊂C∞(M; TM ⊕C) be defined by
(1) N := {(u, p) | Def u = 0 , ∇p = 0} if V = 0 and V0 = 0 on M;
